--- FILE: evaluation/evaluate_metrics.py ---

# evaluation/evaluation_metrics.py
import json
import sys
import os
import time

root_dir = os.path.abspath(os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), '..'))
sys.path.append(root_dir)
sys.path.append(os.path.join(root_dir, 'api')) 

from api.analyzer import analyze_source_code

# --- THE ACADEMIC EQUIVALENCE MAP ---
# This allows the independent ground truth to remain in standard Big-O notation,
# while allowing the analyzer to output precise mathematical recurrence relations.
EQUIVALENCE_MAP = {
    # Recursion Equivalencies
    "T(n) = T(n/2) + O(1)": "O(log n)",
    "T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n)": "O(n log n)",
    "T(n) = T(n-1) + O(1)": "O(n)",
    "T(n) = T(n-1) + O(n)": "O(n^2)",
    "T(n) = T(n-1) + T(n-2) + O(1)": "O(2^n)",
    "T(n) = n * T(n-1)": "O(n!)",
    "T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(1)": "O(n)",
    "T(n) = T(n/2) + O(n)": "O(n)",
    "T(n) = T(n-1) + O(log n)": "O(n log n)",
    
    # Structural Equivalencies (where standard theory assumes n=m)
    "O(n * m)": "O(n^2)",
    "O(n^2 * m)": "O(n^3)",
    "O(n * m^2)": "O(n^3)",
    "O(n^2 log n)": "O(n^2 log n)",
    
    # Amortized Equivalencies
    "O(1) amortized": "O(1)",
    
    # Space Equivalencies
    "O(V)": "O(V + E)"
}

def check_match(actual, expected):
    """Checks if actual matches expected, factoring in mathematical equivalence."""
    if actual == expected:
        return True
    
    # Check if the actual output translates to the expected standard Big-O
    translated_actual = EQUIVALENCE_MAP.get(actual)
    if translated_actual == expected:
        return True
        
    # Check reverse mapping (just in case)
    if EQUIVALENCE_MAP.get(expected) == actual:
        return True
        
    return False

def calculate_metrics():
    dataset_path = os.path.join(os.path.dirname(__file__), 'dataset', 'ground_truth.json')
    with open(dataset_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        dataset = json.load(f)
        
    total_algorithms = len(dataset)
    overall_time_correct = 0
    overall_space_correct = 0
    total_lines_evaluated = 0
    lines_time_correct = 0
    lines_space_correct = 0
    total_processing_time = 0
    
    # Lists to store the labels for Precision, Recall, and F1-score
    y_true_time = []
    y_pred_time = []
    y_true_space = []
    y_pred_space = []
    
    print("Starting Independent AST Complexity Evaluation...\n")

    for item in dataset:
        code_snippet = item['code']
        expected_time = item['expected_overall_time']
        expected_space = item.get('expected_overall_space', 'O(1)')
        
        start_time = time.perf_counter()
        results = analyze_source_code(code_snippet)
        end_time = time.perf_counter()
        
        if results.get("status") == "error":
            print(f"[Error] Failed to analyze {item['name']}: {results.get('message')}")
            continue

        processing_time_ms = (end_time - start_time) * 1000
        total_processing_time += processing_time_ms
        
        actual_time = results.get("total", "O(1)")
        actual_space = results.get("space_total", "O(1)")
        actual_details = results.get("lines", [])
        
        # 1. Validate Overall Time Complexity
        if check_match(actual_time, expected_time):
            overall_time_correct += 1
        else:
            print(f"[Time Mismatch] {item['name']}: Expected {expected_time}, got {actual_time}")
            
        # 2. Validate Overall Space Complexity
        if check_match(actual_space, expected_space):
            overall_space_correct += 1
        else:
            print(f"[Space Mismatch] {item['name']}: Expected {expected_space}, got {actual_space}")
            
        # Store data for Scikit-Learn Metrics
        y_true_time.append(expected_time)
        y_pred_time.append(EQUIVALENCE_MAP.get(actual_time, actual_time))
        
        y_true_space.append(expected_space)
        y_pred_space.append(EQUIVALENCE_MAP.get(actual_space, actual_space))
            
        # 3. Validate Line-Level Complexity
        actual_lines_dict = { detail.get('lineno'): detail for detail in actual_details }
        
        for expected_line in item.get('line_metrics', []):
            total_lines_evaluated += 1
            lineno = expected_line['lineno']
            
            if lineno in actual_lines_dict:
                actual_line = actual_lines_dict[lineno]
                actual_local_time = actual_line.get('local_time')
                actual_global_time = actual_line.get('global_time')
                actual_global_space = actual_line.get('global_space', 'O(1)')
                
                # Check Time Match
                if check_match(actual_local_time, expected_line.get('local_time')) and check_match(actual_global_time, expected_line.get('global_time')):
                    lines_time_correct += 1
                else:
                    print(f"  -> [Time Line {lineno} Mismatch] {item['name']}:")
                    print(f"     Expected Time: Local {expected_line.get('local_time')}, Global {expected_line.get('global_time')}")
                    print(f"     Got Time     : Local {actual_local_time}, Global {actual_global_time}")

                # Check Space Match
                expected_line_space = expected_line.get('space', 'O(1)')
                if check_match(actual_global_space, expected_line_space) or check_match(actual_line.get('local_space', 'O(1)'), expected_line_space):
                    lines_space_correct += 1
                else:
                    print(f"  -> [Space Line {lineno} Mismatch] {item['name']}: Expected {expected_line_space}, got {actual_global_space}")


    # --- CALCULATE SOP 2 METRICS ---
    time_accuracy = (overall_time_correct / total_algorithms) * 100 if total_algorithms > 0 else 0
    space_accuracy = (overall_space_correct / total_algorithms) * 100 if total_algorithms > 0 else 0
    
    line_time_acc = (lines_time_correct / total_lines_evaluated) * 100 if total_lines_evaluated > 0 else 0
    line_space_acc = (lines_space_correct / total_lines_evaluated) * 100 if total_lines_evaluated > 0 else 0
    
    avg_processing_time = total_processing_time / total_algorithms if total_algorithms > 0 else 0
    time_error_rate = 100 - time_accuracy
    space_error_rate = 100 - space_accuracy

    # --- PRINT FINAL REPORT ---
    print("\n" + "="*60)
    print("   SOP 2: ALGORITHM STRUCTURAL ACCURACY")
    print("="*60)
    print(f"Total Algorithms Tested   : {total_algorithms}")
    print(f"Total Lines Evaluated     : {total_lines_evaluated}")
    print("-" * 60)
    print(f"1. Time Complexity Detection Acc  : {time_accuracy:.2f}%")
    print(f"2. Space Complexity Detection Acc : {space_accuracy:.2f}%")
    print(f"3. Line-Level Time Class. Acc     : {line_time_acc:.2f}%")
    print(f"4. Line-Level Space Class. Acc    : {line_space_acc:.2f}%")
    print(f"5. Average Processing Time        : {avg_processing_time:.2f} ms")
    print(f"6. Time Error Rate                : {time_error_rate:.2f}%")
    print(f"7. Space Error Rate               : {space_error_rate:.2f}%")
    
    # --- SCIKIT-LEARN CLASSIFICATION METRICS ---
    print("\n" + "="*60)
    print("   ADVANCED CLASSIFICATION METRICS (Precision/Recall/F1)")
    print("="*60)
    try:
        from sklearn.metrics import classification_report
        print("--- TIME COMPLEXITY REPORT ---")
        time_report = classification_report(y_true_time, y_pred_time, zero_division=0)
        print(time_report)
        
        print("\n--- SPACE COMPLEXITY REPORT ---")
        space_report = classification_report(y_true_space, y_pred_space, zero_division=0)
        print(space_report)
        
    except ImportError:
        print("Note: The 'scikit-learn' library is missing.")
        print("Please install it to view Precision, Recall, and F1-Scores by running:")
        print("pip install scikit-learn")
    print("="*60)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calculate_metrics()

--- FILE: evaluation/evaluation_metrics.py ---

    # --- CALCULATE SOP 2 METRICS ---
    time_accuracy = (overall_time_correct / total_algorithms) * 100 if total_algorithms > 0 else 0
    space_accuracy = (overall_space_correct / total_algorithms) * 100 if total_algorithms > 0 else 0
    
    line_time_acc = (lines_time_correct / total_lines_evaluated) * 100 if total_lines_evaluated > 0 else 0
    line_space_acc = (lines_space_correct / total_lines_evaluated) * 100 if total_lines_evaluated > 0 else 0
    
    avg_processing_time = total_processing_time / total_algorithms if total_algorithms > 0 else 0
    time_error_rate = 100 - time_accuracy
    space_error_rate = 100 - space_accuracy

    # --- PRINT FINAL REPORT ---
    print("\n" + "="*60)
    print("   SOP 2: ALGORITHM STRUCTURAL ACCURACY")
    print("="*60)
    print(f"Total Algorithms Tested   : {total_algorithms}")
    print(f"Total Lines Evaluated     : {total_lines_evaluated}")
    print("-" * 60)
    print(f"1. Time Complexity Detection Acc  : {time_accuracy:.2f}%")
    print(f"2. Space Complexity Detection Acc : {space_accuracy:.2f}%")
    print(f"3. Line-Level Time Class. Acc     : {line_time_acc:.2f}%")
    print(f"4. Line-Level Space Class. Acc    : {line_space_acc:.2f}%")
    print(f"5. Average Processing Time        : {avg_processing_time:.2f} ms")
    print(f"6. Time Error Rate                : {time_error_rate:.2f}%")
    print(f"7. Space Error Rate               : {space_error_rate:.2f}%")
    
    # --- SCIKIT-LEARN CLASSIFICATION METRICS ---
    print("\n" + "="*60)
    print("   ADVANCED CLASSIFICATION METRICS (Precision/Recall/F1)")
    print("="*60)
    try:
        from sklearn.metrics import classification_report
        print("--- TIME COMPLEXITY REPORT ---")
        time_report = classification_report(y_true_time, y_pred_time, zero_division=0)
        print(time_report)
        
        print("\n--- SPACE COMPLEXITY REPORT ---")
        space_report = classification_report(y_true_space, y_pred_space, zero_division=0)
        print(space_report)
        
    except ImportError:
        print("Note: The 'scikit-learn' library is missing.")
        print("Please install it to view Precision, Recall, and F1-Scores by running:")
        print("pip install scikit-learn")
    print("="*60)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    calculate_metrics()

--- FILE: frontend/README.md ---

# React + Vite

This template provides a minimal setup to get React working in Vite with HMR and some ESLint rules.

Currently, two official plugins are available:

- [@vitejs/plugin-react](https://github.com/vitejs/vite-plugin-react/blob/main/packages/plugin-react) uses [Oxc](https://oxc.rs)
- [@vitejs/plugin-react-swc](https://github.com/vitejs/vite-plugin-react/blob/main/packages/plugin-react-swc) uses [SWC](https://swc.rs/)

## React Compiler

The React Compiler is not enabled on this template because of its impact on dev & build performances. To add it, see [this documentation](https://react.dev/learn/react-compiler/installation).

## Expanding the ESLint configuration

If you are developing a production application, we recommend using TypeScript with type-aware lint rules enabled. Check out the [TS template](https://github.com/vitejs/vite/tree/main/packages/create-vite/template-react-ts) for information on how to integrate TypeScript and [`typescript-eslint`](https://typescript-eslint.io) in your project.

import js from '@eslint/js'

  globalIgnores(['dist']),
  {
    files: ['**/*.{js,jsx}'],
    extends: [
      js.configs.recommended,
      reactHooks.configs.flat.recommended,
      reactRefresh.configs.vite,
    ],

      ecmaVersion: 2020,
      globals: globals.browser,
      parserOptions: {
        ecmaVersion: 'latest',
        ecmaFeatures: { jsx: true },
        sourceType: 'module',
      },
    },
    rules: {
      'no-unused-vars': ['error', { varsIgnorePattern: '^[A-Z_]' }],
    },
  },
])

      "id": "m3_l2_a1",
      "title": "Halving the Search (Binary Search)",
      "task": [
        "If a list is perfectly sorted, you don't need to check every item! You can check the exact middle. If the target is smaller, you throw away the entire right half of the list. This is incredibly fast!",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Define `binary_search(arr, target)`.",
        "2. Create two pointer variables: `low = 0` and `high = len(arr) - 1`.",
        "3. Make a `while` loop that runs as long as `low <= high`.",
        "4. Inside, calculate the middle: `mid = (low + high) // 2` (use floor division!).",
        "5. If `arr[mid] == target`, return `mid`. If `arr[mid] < target`, change `low` to `mid + 1`. Otherwise, change `high` to `mid - 1`.",
        "6. Return `-1` at the very end.",
        "",
        "Sample Outputs:",
        "binary_search([10, 20, 30, 40], 30) â†’ 2",
        "binary_search([1, 2, 3, 4], 1) â†’ 0"
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(log n)",
      "targetSpace": "O(1)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "binary_search([10, 20, 30, 40], 30)", "expected": "2", "isHidden": false },

        { "call": "binary_search([5, 10, 15], 20)", "expected": "-1", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "binary_search([], 5)", "expected": "-1", "isHidden": true },
        { "call": "binary_search([7, 14, 21, 28], 14)", "expected": "1", "isHidden": true }

  "optimizations": [
    {
      "id": "m3_opt_1",
      "title": "Optimization Challenge: Slow to Fast Search",
      "task": [
        "A linear scan (O(n)) through a sorted list is highly inefficient for large datasets. Refactoring the traversal into a Binary Search structure reduces the time it takes to a rapid O(log n).",
        "",
        "Optimization Guidelines:",
        "1. Analyze the current implementation: the `for` loop checks every single item.",
        "2. Delete the `for` loop entirely.",
        "3. Introduce `low` and `high` pointer variables to establish your search boundaries.",
        "4. Implement a `while` loop that calculates the middle. Adjust the boundaries to systematically halve the search space.",
        "5. Observe the analytical metrics demonstrating the transition to O(log n) efficiency.",
        "",
        "Sample Outputs:",
        "search([1, 3, 5, 7, 9], 7) â†’ 3",

    {
      "id": "m3_opt_2",
      "title": "Optimization Challenge: Finding the Peak",
      "task": [
        "A peak element is an integer strictly greater than its neighbors. Instead of an O(n) sequential scan, we can deploy a modified binary search to intuitively follow the slope uphill in O(log n) time.",
        "",
        "Optimization Guidelines:",
        "1. Eliminate the inefficient O(n) sequential loop.",
        "2. Establish `low` and `high` pointer variables.",
        "3. Within a `while low < high` loop, compute the midpoint index.",
        "4. Evaluate the slope: if `arr[mid] < arr[mid + 1]`, the slope is going UP to the right. Adjust the `low` pointer up to `mid + 1`.",
        "5. If the slope is going DOWN, the peak is in the left half. Reduce the `high` pointer to `mid`.",
        "6. Return the `low` pointer index when the loop concludes.",
        "",
        "Sample Outputs:",
        "find_peak([1, 2, 3, 1]) â†’ 2",
        "find_peak([1, 2, 1, 3, 5, 6, 4]) â†’ 5"
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(log n)",
      "targetSpace": "O(1)",
      "templateUrl": "/data/optimizations/m3-3.2.json",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "find_peak([1, 2, 3, 1])", "expected": "2", "isHidden": false },

        { "call": "find_peak([10, 9, 8, 7])", "expected": "0", "isHidden": true },
        { "call": "find_peak([5])", "expected": "0", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m3_opt_3",
      "title": "Optimization Challenge: Avoiding the Call Stack",
      "task": [
        "When you run a recursive Binary Search, you leave a trail of memory behind for every function call. Converting the recursion into a standard `while` loop optimizes this to O(1) space.",
        "",
        "Optimization Guidelines:",
        "1. Identify the memory stack generated by returning the function within itself (e.g., `return search_optimized(...)`).",
        "2. Remove all recursive function calls from the structure.",
        "3. Refactor the core logic using an iterative `while low <= high:` loop structure.",
        "4. Reassign the `low` and `high` pointer variables directly within the loop instead of passing them as parameters to a new function.",
        "5. Verify through the analyzer that the Space Complexity has successfully dropped to O(1).",
        "",

        "5. If `char_t` is in `map_t` but its saved value isn't `char_s`, return `False`.",

{
  "lesson_1": [
    {
      "id": "m6_l1_a1",
      "title": "Backtracking Architecture: The Power Set",
      "task": [
        "Backtracking is like exploring a branching tree. You make a choice, explore where it leads, and then 'backtrack' (undo the choice) to try a different path. Let's find every possible subset of a list!",
        "",
        "Phase 1: The Setup & Base Case",
        "Define `power_set(arr)`. Inside it, create an empty `results` list. Then define a helper function `backtrack(start_index, current_path)`. The Base Case is simple: instantly append a copy of `current_path` to `results` every single time the function runs.",
        "",
        "Phase 2: The Loop (Making Choices)",
        "Inside `backtrack`, make a 'count with i' loop that starts at `start_index` and goes to the end of `arr`. Inside the loop, append `arr[i]` to your `current_path`. You have now 'chosen' this item!",
        "",
        "Phase 3: The Recursive Dive",
        "Call `backtrack(i + 1, current_path)` so the function dives deeper into the tree to explore what happens when this item is included.",
        "",
        "Phase 4: The Backtrack (Undoing Choices)",
        "After the recursive call finishes, you MUST pop the last item off `current_path`. This 'undoes' your choice so the loop can move on and try the next item!"
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: You must append a COPY of your path to the results (e.g., `list(current_path)` or `current_path[:]`). If you don't, Python just links to the exact same list, and it will end up empty at the end!",
        "Hint 2: The magic happens in Phase 4. By popping the item off, you revert the list back to exactly how it looked before the loop started."
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(2^n)",
      "targetSpace": "O(n)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "power_set([1, 2])", "expected": "[[], [1], [1, 2], [2]]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "power_set([1])", "expected": "[[], [1]]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "power_set([])", "expected": "[[]]", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m6_l1_a2",
      "title": "Subsets with a Target Sum",
      "task": [
        "Now that we know the Backtracking Architecture (Choose, Dive, Undo), let's use it to only find subsets that add up to a specific target number.",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Define `target_subsets(arr, target)`. Create your `results` list and `backtrack(start_index, current_path, current_sum)` helper.",
        "2. Base Case: If `current_sum == target`, append a copy of `current_path` to `results`.",
        "3. Pruning: If `current_sum > target`, just `return` immediately! (This stops the tree from growing down a dead end).",
        "4. Loop `i` from `start_index` to the end. Append `arr[i]` to the path.",
        "5. Dive: Call `backtrack(i + 1, current_path, current_sum + arr[i])`.",
        "6. Undo: Pop the last item off the path."
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: 'Pruning' is when you stop a recursive dive early because you know it's already failed. Since all our numbers are positive, if the sum goes over the target, it can never come back down!",
        "Hint 2: Follow the exact same Choose -> Dive -> Undo pattern from the first activity."
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(2^n)",
      "targetSpace": "O(n)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "target_subsets([2, 3, 6, 7], 7)", "expected": "[[7]]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "target_subsets([2, 3, 5], 8)", "expected": "[[3, 5]]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "target_subsets([1, 2, 3], 6)", "expected": "[[1, 2, 3]]", "isHidden": true }
      ]

      "title": "Permutations Architecture",
      "task": [
        "Permutations are different from subsets. In permutations, the order matters! [1, 2] is different from [2, 1]. Because we can pick numbers in any order, our loop always starts at 0, but we need to track what we have already used.",
        "",
        "Phase 1: The Base Case",
        "Define `permute(arr)` and `backtrack(path, used)`. Base Case: If `len(path) == len(arr)`, append a copy of the path to results and return.",
        "",
        "Phase 2: State Tracking & The Loop",
        "Loop `i` from 0 to the end of the list. If `arr[i]` is already inside our `used` Set (or list), `continue` (skip it!).",
        "",
        "Phase 3 & 4: Choose, Dive, Undo",
        "Add `arr[i]` to `path` AND add it to the `used` Set. Dive with `backtrack(path, used)`. Undo by removing `arr[i]` from BOTH `path` and `used`."
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: In subsets, we move our loop forward (`i + 1`) to avoid going backwards. In permutations, we WANT to go backwards, so the loop always starts at 0.",
        "Hint 2: Because the loop always starts at 0, we must use a 'used' tracker so we don't accidentally pick the exact same item twice in the same path."
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(n!)",
      "targetSpace": "O(n)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "permute([1, 2])", "expected": "[[1, 2], [2, 1]]", "isHidden": false },

      ],
      "hints": [

      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m6_l4_a2",
      "title": "DP Matrix: Filling Coin Change",
      "task": [
        "Now let's populate the matrix. For every cell, we ask: 'Is it better to NOT use this coin (pull the answer from the row above), or to USE this coin (pull the answer from a few columns back)?'",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Define `coin_change(coins, amount)`. Initialize your `dp` matrix filled with `99999`, and set column 0 to `0`.",
        "2. Loop `i` from 1 to `len(coins) + 1` (rows). Loop `j` from 1 to `amount + 1` (cols).",
        "3. Grab the current coin: `coin = coins[i-1]`.",
        "4. If `coin <= j` (it fits!), the cell equals the minimum of: the cell above (`dp[i-1][j]`), OR 1 + the cell shifted back by the coin amount (`dp[i][j - coin]`).",
        "5. Else (it doesn't fit), just copy the cell directly above.",
        "6. Return `dp[-1][-1]`. If it is still `99999`, return `-1`."
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: `dp[i-1][j]` represents 'What was the best answer before I had access to this new coin?'.",
        "Hint 2: `dp[i][j - coin]` represents 'If I use this coin, what was the best answer for the remaining leftover amount?'"
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(c * a)",
      "targetSpace": "O(c * a)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "coin_change([1, 2, 5], 11)", "expected": "3", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "coin_change([2], 3)", "expected": "-1", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "coin_change([1], 0)", "expected": "0", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m6_l4_a3",
      "title": "DP Matrix: Knapsack Initialization",
      "task": [
        "The 0/1 Knapsack problem maximizes value without going over a weight limit. Let's initialize a matrix where rows are items, and columns are weight capacities from 0 to W.",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Define `init_knapsack(weights, values, max_weight)`.",
        "2. Create a list of lists with `len(weights) + 1` rows, and `max_weight + 1` columns.",
        "3. Fill the entire matrix with `0`. (Because 0 weight capacity gives 0 value, and 0 items available gives 0 value).",
        "4. Return the `dp` matrix."
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: This initialization is much easier than Coin Change because we are maximizing value (starting from 0) rather than minimizing coins (starting from infinity).",
        "Hint 2: Matrix visual for 1 item, max_weight=2:\nRow 0 (No items): [0, 0, 0]\nRow 1 (Item 1):   [0, 0, 0]"
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(i * w)",
      "targetSpace": "O(i * w)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "init_knapsack([1], [10], 2)", "expected": "[[0, 0, 0], [0, 0, 0]]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "init_knapsack([2, 3], [10, 20], 1)", "expected": "[[0, 0], [0, 0], [0, 0]]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "init_knapsack([], [], 0)", "expected": "[[0]]", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m6_l4_a4",
      "title": "DP Matrix: Filling the Knapsack",
      "task": [
        "Let's fill the Knapsack matrix. For every cell, we ask: 'Is it better to LEAVE this item (take the value from the row above), or TAKE this item (add its value to the leftover weight capacity)?'",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Define `knapsack(weights, values, max_weight)`. Initialize your `dp` matrix of zeroes.",
        "2. Loop `i` from 1 to `len(weights) + 1`. Loop `w` from 1 to `max_weight + 1`.",
        "3. If `weights[i-1] <= w` (it fits!): Set `dp[i][w]` to the MAX of: cell above (`dp[i-1][w]`), OR `values[i-1]` + cell above shifted left by weight (`dp[i-1][w - weights[i-1]]`).",
        "4. Else: Set `dp[i][w]` to the cell directly above.",
        "5. Return the bottom-right cell: `dp[-1][-1]`."
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: `dp[i-1][w]` means 'Leave the item'. You keep the same weight column, but step up to the previous row's value.",
        "Hint 2: `values[i-1] + dp[i-1][w - weights[i-1]]` means 'Take the item'. You add its value, and step back in the matrix by the item's weight to see what else you could afford with the leftover capacity."
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(i * w)",
      "targetSpace": "O(i * w)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "knapsack([1, 2, 3], [10, 15, 40], 6)", "expected": "65", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack([2, 3, 4], [1, 2, 3], 1)", "expected": "0", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack([1, 1], [10, 20], 1)", "expected": "20", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m6_l4_a5",
      "title": "DP Matrix: Tracing Back Choices",
      "task": [
        "The matrix gives us the max value, but it doesn't tell us WHICH items we packed! To find out, we have to start at the bottom-right cell and walk backwards through the matrix.",
        "",
        "Instructions:",
        "1. Define `knapsack_items(weights, values, max_weight)`. Generate your fully filled `dp` matrix.",
        "2. Create an empty `chosen_items` list. Set `r = len(weights)` and `c = max_weight`.",
        "3. Loop `while r > 0 and c > 0`.",
        "4. If `dp[r][c] != dp[r-1][c]`, the value changed! This means we TOOK item `r-1`. Append `r-1` to `chosen_items` and subtract its weight from `c`.",
        "5. Subtract 1 from `r` (move up a row).",
        "6. Return the `chosen_items` array."
      ],
      "hints": [
        "Hint 1: If `dp[r][c] == dp[r-1][c]`, it means the value is exactly the same as the row above. We clearly left the item behind, so we just step up one row.",
        "Hint 2: We only append to our chosen list when we detect a mathematical difference between the current row and the row above."
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(i * w)",
      "targetSpace": "O(i * w)",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "knapsack_items([1, 2, 3], [10, 15, 40], 6)", "expected": "[2, 1, 0]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack_items([10, 20], [50, 60], 5)", "expected": "[]", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack_items([1, 4, 3], [15, 30, 45], 4)", "expected": "[2, 0]", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    }

    {
      "id": "m6_opt_1",
      "title": "Optimization Challenge: Memoizing the Matrix",
      "task": [
        "Building a massive 2D matrix uses a lot of memory. If we only need the final answer, we can use a Top-Down Recursive approach and a Dictionary (`memo`) to only calculate the cells we actually need!",
        "",
        "Optimization Guidelines:",
        "1. Define `knapsack_memo(weights, values, capacity, index, memo)`. The `memo` starts as `{}`.",
        "2. Base Case: If `index < 0` or `capacity == 0`, return `0`.",
        "3. Check Cache: If `(index, capacity)` is in `memo`, return it instantly!",
        "4. If `weights[index] > capacity`, the item is too heavy. Save and return the recursive call for `index - 1`.",
        "5. Otherwise, calculate `leave_it` (index - 1) and `take_it` (value + index - 1 with reduced capacity). Save the MAX of these to `memo`, and return it."
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(i * w)",
      "targetSpace": "O(i * w)",
      "templateUrl": "/data/optimizations/m6-6.1.json",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "knapsack_memo([1, 2, 3], [10, 15, 40], 6, 2, {})", "expected": "65", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack_memo([2, 3, 4], [1, 2, 3], 1, 2, {})", "expected": "0", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack_memo([1, 1], [10, 20], 1, 1, {})", "expected": "20", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    },
    {
      "id": "m6_opt_2",
      "title": "Optimization Challenge: 1D Array Space Compression",
      "task": [
        "In the DP Matrix for Knapsack, every row only ever looks at the row directly above it! That means we don't need a massive 2D Grid. We can compress it into a single 1D array to save massive memory.",
        "",
        "Optimization Guidelines:",
        "1. Define `knapsack_1d(weights, values, max_weight)`.",
        "2. Create a single 1D array `dp` of zeroes, size `max_weight + 1`.",
        "3. Loop `i` over the items. Loop `w` BACKWARDS from `max_weight` down to `weights[i]`. (Looping backwards is critical so we don't overwrite values we need for the current row!).",
        "4. Update the cell: `dp[w] = max(dp[w], values[i] + dp[w - weights[i]])`.",
        "5. Return `dp[-1]`. Your Space Complexity is now a tiny O(W) instead of O(N*W)!"
      ],
      "targetTime": "O(i * w)",
      "targetSpace": "O(w)",
      "templateUrl": "/data/optimizations/m6-6.2.json",
      "testCasesPool": [
        { "call": "knapsack_1d([1, 2, 3], [10, 15, 40], 6)", "expected": "65", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack_1d([2, 3, 4], [1, 2, 3], 1)", "expected": "0", "isHidden": false },
        { "call": "knapsack_1d([1, 1], [10, 20], 1)", "expected": "20", "isHidden": true }
      ]
    }
  ]
